import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.signalprocessing import DCT
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import omp
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# synthetic setup: smooth periodic signal, sparse in the DCT-II basis
rng = np.random.default_rng(7)
n = 512
t = np.arange(n)
freqs = rng.choice(np.arange(5, 200), 12, replace=False)
amps = rng.uniform(0.5, 2, 12) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 12)
xtrue = sum(a * np.cos(np.pi * f * (2 * t + 1) / (2 * n)) for a, f in zip(amps, freqs))

# only 128 randomly located samples were recorded
iava = np.sort(rng.choice(n, 128, replace=False))
R = pylops.Restriction(n, iava)
y = R @ xtrue + 0.005 * rng.standard_normal(128)

# model = DCT coefficients; signal = D.H @ model (orthonormal DCT-II)
D = DCT(n, type=2)
Op = R @ D.H

# greedy sparse recovery: the signal has only 12 nonzero DCT coefficients
minv, nnz_iter, _ = omp(Op, y, niter_outer=12, sigma=1e-6)

# full 512-sample reconstructed signal
xinv = D.H @ minv

print(f"nonzero DCT coefficients: {np.sum(np.abs(minv) > 1e-6)}")
print(f"missing samples reconstructed: {n - len(iava)}")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import fista
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

rng = np.random.default_rng(7)
n = 512
t = np.arange(n)
freqs = rng.choice(np.arange(5, 200), 12, replace=False)
amps = rng.uniform(0.5, 2, 12) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 12)
xtrue = sum(a * np.cos(np.pi * f * (2 * t + 1) / (2 * n)) for a, f in zip(amps, freqs))

# only 128 randomly located samples were recorded
iava = np.sort(rng.choice(n, 128, replace=False))
R = pylops.Restriction(n, iava)
y = R @ xtrue + 0.005 * rng.standard_normal(128)

# xtrue is exactly a sparse combination of DCT-II atoms, so model the signal
# as x = D^H c with c sparse, where D is the orthonormal DCT-II
D = pylops.signalprocessing.DCT(n)
assert np.allclose(D.H @ (D @ xtrue), xtrue)

# synthesis operator: coefficients -> samples at the recorded locations
Op = R @ D.H

# FISTA with soft thresholding; eps (the l1 weight) is a small fraction of
# the largest back-projected data value, chosen by hand (not tuned via noise level)
eps = 0.5 * np.max(np.abs(Op.H @ y)) * 0.02
cinv, niter_done, _ = fista(Op, y, niter=2000, eps=eps, tol=1e-12)

xinv = D.H @ cinv
print(f"FISTA iterations: {niter_done}, eps: {eps:.4g}, "
      f"nonzero coeffs: {np.sum(np.abs(cinv) > 1e-2)} (true: 12)")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

import pylops
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import fista
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

rng = np.random.default_rng(7)
n = 512
t = np.arange(n)
freqs = rng.choice(np.arange(5, 200), 12, replace=False)
amps = rng.uniform(0.5, 2, 12) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 12)

y = R @ xtrue + 0.005 * rng.standard_normal(128)

# The true signal is sparse in the DCT-II basis (each term is one basis function),
# so we model x = D^H c with D the orthonormal DCT and solve for a sparse c.

eps = 0.01 * np.max(np.abs(Op.H @ y))

xinv = D.H @ c

print(f"eps={eps:.4g}")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

## Diagnosis: the adjoint is wrong

The operator is a causal, truncated convolution: `y[i] = Σ_j h[i-j] x[j]` for `i < nt`. Its transpose is a **correlation** (anti-causal), `(Aᵀy)[j] = Σ_{i≥j} h[i-j] y[i]`. Your `adjoint` runs a causal convolution again, which is the adjoint of a different operator. The two only agree when `h` is symmetric, and yours is not.

**Check 1, adjoint (dot-test): run, fails.** Ran in PyLops 2.8.0, NumPy 2.0.2, SciPy 1.13.1:

```
Dot test failed, v^H(Opu)=-51.37397917067636 - u^H(Op^Hv)=44.16961557643362
dottest: False
```

The operator has no blocks, so there is nothing to walk with `dottest_blocks`.

**Evidence from the explicit matrix (run):** I built the dense matrix `A` column by column and compared `Aᵀ` with each candidate adjoint:

- `max|Aᵀ − user_adj| = 0.846`. The relative error on a random `y` is 1.27, so the adjoint is not even close.
- `max|Aᵀ − fixed_adj| = 0.0`, and the dot-test passes with the fix.

**Effect on cgls (run):** with your exact setup (`niter=100, damp=1e-3`, same `xtrue` and noise):

| adjoint | istop | iters | rel. error of `xinv` | cost first → last |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| yours | 2 | 100 | 3.19 | 3.00 → 20.37 |
| corrected | 1 | 34 | 0.016 | 3.00 → 0.0051 |

With the wrong adjoint, the cost does not decrease at all. It rises to about 20 and the model is about five times the size of the truth. That is what you saw as a stall, and it is what a non-adjoint pair does to any gradient-based solver. The corrected operator converges to a residual of 0.0017 relative to `‖y‖`.

## Fix

```python
def adjoint(y):
    # transpose of causal convolution = correlation: flip, convolve, flip back
    return np.convolve(y[::-1], h, mode="full")[:nt][::-1]
```

Tested: the dot-test passes, and cgls converges to a 1.6% model error. The other part of the operator (`forward`) is unchanged.

## Other checks

- **Shapes and ordering (run in effect):** `Op.shape` is `(300, 300)` and the data is a 1-D array of length `nt`. The printed shapes match.
- **dtypes (run in effect):** the operator and data are all `float64`. No complex data is involved.
- **NaN/Inf (not explicitly checked):** the printed norms are finite, but I did not run `np.isfinite` on each array.
- **Scaling and conditioning (not run):** I did not run `Op.eigs` or `Op.cond()`. The filter has `Σ|h| ≈ 6`, so the operator norm is O(1) and scaling is unlikely to be the issue, but that is an estimate, not a measurement.
- **Solver choice, regularisation, modelling error (not run as separate checks):** with the corrected adjoint, the remaining error is small and the residual sits at the noise level, so I did not pursue them.

Result: the wrong adjoint is the cause, and the corrected version passes the dot-test and gives a good model with cgls.

The bug is the adjoint. `adjoint` applies the same causal convolution as `forward`, so the operator is only self-adjoint when `h` is symmetric. Your `h` is one-sided, so `Op.H` is not the transpose of `Op`. cgls then descends a direction that isn't the gradient of the least-squares cost, which is why it stalls at a wrong model.

The transpose of a causal, truncated convolution is a correlation: `(Aᵀy)[m] = Σₖ h[k]·y[m+k]`. You get that by flipping `y`, convolving, and flipping back:

```python
def adjoint(y):
    return np.convolve(y[::-1], h, mode="full")[:nt][::-1]
```

I checked this by running the code. Results with your adjoint versus the corrected one:

| `pylops.utils.dottest(Op, 300, 300)` | fails (AssertionError) | passes |
| cgls relative model error | 3.19 | 0.016 |

The corrected version recovers the spikes with about 1.6% relative error. The remaining error comes from the noise and `damp`, not from the operator.

Your call to `dottest` in the question will work once the adjoint is fixed. It doesn't accept a `tol` keyword in this PyLops version, so don't pass one.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The problem is the adjoint. `adjoint` applies the same causal convolution as `forward`, so the operator isn't its own transpose. CGLS assumes the adjoint is the transpose, so its updates are wrong and it stalls. The dot test catches this.

**Why the adjoint is wrong**

The forward operator is a lower-triangular Toeplitz matrix, `y[i] = sum_j h[i-j] x[j]` for `j <= i`. Its transpose is `x_adj[j] = sum_{i>=j} h[i-j] y[i]`, which is an anti-causal correlation. `np.convolve(y, h)` is causal, so it computes the forward operator again, not its transpose. It only matches the transpose when the matrix is symmetric, and this one isn't.

**Fix**

Reverse the input and the output around the convolution:

```python
def adjoint(y):
    return np.convolve(y[::-1], h, mode="full")[:nt][::-1]
```

**Verification**

I ran your setup with this adjoint in the sandbox:

- `pylops.utils.dottest(Op, nt, nt)` with your original adjoint returns `False`. With the fixed adjoint it returns `True`. Note that `dottest` raises `AssertionError` by default, which is why your original call failed loudly; I passed `raiseerror=False` to get a boolean.
- With the fixed adjoint, `cgls(..., niter=100, damp=1e-3)` gives a relative error of about 0.016 against `xtrue`.

Your regularization and noise level were fine. The stall came only from the wrong adjoint.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The bug is the adjoint. `adjoint` applies the same causal convolution as `forward`, so it computes `T @ y` instead of `T.T @ y`. CGLS assumes the adjoint is the transpose, so it descends along the wrong directions. That's why the cost stalls and the dot test fails.

For a causal filter `y[n] = sum_k h[k] x[n-k]`, the transpose is a correlation (the time-reversed convolution):

```python
def adjoint(y):
    return np.convolve(y[::-1], h, mode="full")[:nt][::-1]
```

I checked this with NumPy using random vectors and a dense matrix. The dot test `<Ax, v> == <x, A^T v>` gives 74.89 on both sides with the corrected adjoint, and the original gives 74.89 vs -33.35. The corrected adjoint also matches the dense `A.T` exactly. I didn't run pylops itself, so run `pylops.utils.dottest(Op, nt, nt)` to confirm it passes in your environment.

Two smaller notes:
- `dottest` takes the operator dimensions (`nr, nc`), and `Op.shape` is `(300, 300)` here, so the call you wrote is fine.
- If you'd rather not hand-write the adjoint, `pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nt, h, offset=0)` implements a causal convolution with a correct adjoint. I haven't verified its signature against your installed pylops version, so check the docs first.

judge votes: PASS PASS PASS

Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The bug is in `adjoint`. It applies the same causal convolution as `forward`, but the adjoint of a causal convolution is a correlation, which runs the other way.

`forward` multiplies by a lower-triangular Toeplitz matrix `H`. Its transpose `Hᵀ` is upper-triangular, so `Hᵀy` has to accumulate `y[j]·h[j-k]` for `j ≥ k`. Convolving `y` again gives `H·y`, not `Hᵀy`. CGLS then solves the normal equations with the wrong `AᵀA`, which is why the cost stalls and the model is wrong.

The fix is to reverse, convolve, and reverse back:

```python
def adjoint(y):
    return np.convolve(y[::-1], h, mode="full")[:nt][::-1]
```

I ran your script with this change. The results:

| Version | Dot test | CGLS relative error |
|---|---|---|
| Original adjoint | fails (AssertionError) | 3.19 |
| Corrected adjoint | passes | 0.016 |
| `pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nt, h, offset=0)` | — | 0.016 |

The forward operator itself was fine. The filter's unit-diagonal lower-triangular Toeplitz form is well-conditioned here, so the inversion works once the adjoint is correct.

If you don't need a custom operator, `Convolve1D` is the cleaner route: it has a correct adjoint built in, and it gives the same answer as the fixed `FunctionOperator`. Use `dottest` on any custom operator before running a solver.

Prompt

I wrapped my causal filter as a PyLops FunctionOperator to deconvolve with cgls, but cgls stalls: the cost barely decreases after the first few iterations and the model looks nothing like the truth. pylops.utils.dottest(Op, 300, 300) also fails. What's wrong with my code?

import numpy as np
import pylops

nt = 300
h = np.exp(-np.arange(30) / 6.0)  # causal, decaying filter

def forward(x):
    return np.convolve(x, h, mode="full")[:nt]

def adjoint(y):
    return np.convolve(y, h, mode="full")[:nt]

Op = pylops.FunctionOperator(forward, adjoint, nt, nt)

rng = np.random.default_rng(3)
xtrue = np.zeros(nt)
xtrue[[40, 90, 150, 220]] = [1.0, -0.7, 0.5, 0.9]
y = Op @ xtrue + 1e-3 * rng.standard_normal(nt)

xinv = pylops.cgls(Op, y, x0=np.zeros(nt), niter=100, damp=1e-3)[0]
print(np.linalg.norm(xinv - xtrue) / np.linalg.norm(xtrue))
Graders — what "good" means for this case
answerllm

Ground truth: the forward operator is a causal convolution (np.convolve(x, h, "full")[:nt]), but the adjoint repeats the same convolution instead of correlating: the adjoint must use the time-reversed filter, e.g. np.convolve(y, h[::-1], "full")[len(h) - 1 : len(h) - 1 + nt] (or np.correlate(y, h, "full") with the matching slice), or the user can use pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D directly. With a correct adjoint cgls converges.

PASS only if ALL of these hold:

  • It says the adjoint is wrong (not the transpose of the forward) and that this is why the dot-test fails and cgls stalls.
  • It identifies the missing time reversal / correlation (and the corresponding slice) and gives a corrected adjoint equivalent to the ground truth, or recommends Convolve1D.
  • It does not name the solver choice, damp, niter or noise as the main cause.

Otherwise FAIL.

PyLops has no built-in L-curve / GCV /\ndiscrepancy helper, so the skill provides `reference/lcurve_template.py`.\n\nRequires a working inversion (operator passes the dot-test, solver runs). If there\nis none, use `solve-inverse-problem` first.\n\n## 0. Check the environment\n\nSame as the other PyLops skills: use the project's Python runner, check\n`pylops.__version__`, inspect solver signatures in the installed version.\n\n## 1. Identify the parameter(s)\n\nMap the user's solver to the knob being tuned and its meaning:\n\n| Solver | Parameter | Objective term |\n|---|---|---|\n| `cgls` / `lsqr` | `damp` | `damp² ‖x‖²` |\n| `regularized_inversion` | `epsRs=[ε₁, ε₂, …]` | `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` |\n| `normal_equations_inversion` | `epsI`, `epsRs` (and `epsNRs`) | `epsI² ‖x‖²`, `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` (same scaling as `regularized_inversion`) |\n| `ista` / `fista` | `eps` | `eps ‖SOpᴴ x‖₁` (effective threshold is `eps·alpha/2`) |\n| `splitbregman` | `epsRL1s`, `epsRL2s`, `mu` | L1 and L2 weights, data weight |\n| `spgl1` | `sigma` (BPDN) or `tau` (LASSO) | residual bound or L1-ball radius |\n| any Krylov solver without explicit regularisation | `niter` | early stopping (semi-convergence) |\n\nWith more than one weight, tune one at a time (fix the others at a\nscale-based guess), or fix their ratio and sweep an overall factor. Do not\nlaunch a full grid over several parameters unless the problem is small.\n\n## 2. Pick the criterion\n\nChoose based on what the user knows:\n\n1. **Known noise level** (sigma per sample, or an estimate from a noise-only\n   window): **discrepancy principle** - choose the largest regularisation whose\n   data residual `‖y − Op x‖` is ≤ `τ · σ · sqrt(N)`, `τ ≈ 1.0–1.1`. For\n   `spgl1`, this is exactly `sigma = τ σ sqrt(N)`.\n2. **Synthetic with a true model**: maximise `pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, x)`\n   (report it, but warn it does not transfer 1:1 to field data).\n3. **Nothing known**: **L-curve** - plot `log ‖Rx‖` vs `log ‖y − Op x‖` and pick the\n   point of maximum curvature. Needs a wide enough range to show both branches.\n4. **Small problem** (`Op.todense()` affordable, N, M ≲ 3000) and quadratic\n   regularisation: **GCV** computed exactly on the dense matrices (implemented in\n   the template).\n\nReport more than one criterion when they are cheap; disagreement between them is\ninformation for the user.\n\n## 3. Write and run the sweep\n\nAdapt `reference/lcurve_template.py`:\n\n- replace `build_problem` with the user's operator, data and regularisation;\n- replace `solve(eps, x0)` with the user's solver call **with everything else\n  fixed** (same `niter`, tolerance, `x0` policy);\n- use a **log-spaced grid** spanning at least 4 orders of magnitude around a\n  scale-based guess (e.g. `ε₀ ≈ ‖Opᴴ y‖ / ‖Rᴴ R Opᴴ y‖` for quadratic terms, or\n  `eps₀ ≈ max|Opᴴ y|` scale for L1 thresholds, where larger values give all-zero\n  models);\n- solve from large to small ε and **warm start** from the previous solution\n  (`x0=...`) to save iterations - unless the solver ignores `x0`;\n- keep `niter` large enough that each solve has converged, otherwise the curve\n  measures iteration count, not regularisation (check a couple of cost curves);\n- for early stopping as regulariser, run once with a callback that stores\n  `x` every k iterations and compute the same criteria on iteration number.\n\nRun it, then look at the plot before trusting the automatic pick: an L-curve with\nno clear corner (common with L1 terms or very noisy data) should be reported as\nsuch, not forced. With derivative regularisers a second knee often appears at\nvery large ε (the model collapses into the null space of `R`, e.g. a linear\ntrend for `SecondDerivative`); it bends the other way and is not the L-curve\ncorner – the template's curvature orientation already excludes it.\n\n## 4. Hand off\n\nReport:\n\n- the parameter swept, the range and the number of solves;\n- the value picked by each criterion and the one recommended, with the reason;\n- the plot file(s) (L-curve, residual vs ε, SNR vs ε if available) and the\n  model at the chosen value next to one over- and one under-regularised model;\n- the exact line to change in the user's inversion script.\n\n\nARGUMENTS: User's smooth-model inversion with regularized_inversion, Convolve1D forward operator, SecondDerivative regularizer, epsRs=[1.0] picked by eye. Wants an L-curve over epsRs and a recommended value."}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"1fc74e16-65e9-409d-be48-b764f9e05483","uuid":"142d92d0-4efd-499d-bd09-edb653230721","timestamp":"2026-10-08T22:38:02.990Z","isSynthetic":true}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /Users/mrava87/Desktop/OpenSource/pylops-agent-skills/plugins/pylops/skills/tune-regularization\n\nGoal: a **sweep script** in the user's project that evaluates a regularisation\nparameter over a range, applies one or more selection criteria, and reports the\nchosen value with a plot that justifies it. PyLops has no built-in L-curve / GCV /\ndiscrepancy helper, so the skill provides `reference/lcurve_template.py`.\n\nRequires a working inversion (operator passes the dot-test, solver runs). If there\nis none, use `solve-inverse-problem` first.\n\n## 0. Check the environment\n\nSame as the other PyLops skills: use the project's Python runner, check\n`pylops.__version__`, inspect solver signatures in the installed version.\n\n## 1. Identify the parameter(s)\n\nMap the user's solver to the knob being tuned and its meaning:\n\n| Solver | Parameter | Objective term |\n|---|---|---|\n| `cgls` / `lsqr` | `damp` | `damp² ‖x‖²` |\n| `regularized_inversion` | `epsRs=[ε₁, ε₂, …]` | `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` |\n| `normal_equations_inversion` | `epsI`, `epsRs` (and `epsNRs`) | `epsI² ‖x‖²`, `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` (same scaling as `regularized_inversion`) |\n| `ista` / `fista` | `eps` | `eps ‖SOpᴴ x‖₁` (effective threshold is `eps·alpha/2`) |\n| `splitbregman` | `epsRL1s`, `epsRL2s`, `mu` | L1 and L2 weights, data weight |\n| `spgl1` | `sigma` (BPDN) or `tau` (LASSO) | residual bound or L1-ball radius |\n| any Krylov solver without explicit regularisation | `niter` | early stopping (semi-convergence) |\n\nWith more than one weight, tune one at a time (fix the others at a\nscale-based guess), or fix their ratio and sweep an overall factor. Do not\nlaunch a full grid over several parameters unless the problem is small.\n\n## 2. Pick the criterion\n\nChoose based on what the user knows:\n\n1. **Known noise level** (sigma per sample, or an estimate from a noise-only\n   window): **discrepancy principle** - choose the largest regularisation whose\n   data residual `‖y − Op x‖` is ≤ `τ · σ · sqrt(N)`, `τ ≈ 1.0–1.1`. For\n   `spgl1`, this is exactly `sigma = τ σ sqrt(N)`.\n2. **Synthetic with a true model**: maximise `pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, x)`\n   (report it, but warn it does not transfer 1:1 to field data).\n3. **Nothing known**: **L-curve** - plot `log ‖Rx‖` vs `log ‖y − Op x‖` and pick the\n   point of maximum curvature. Needs a wide enough range to show both branches.\n4. **Small problem** (`Op.todense()` affordable, N, M ≲ 3000) and quadratic\n   regularisation: **GCV** computed exactly on the dense matrices (implemented in\n   the template).\n\nReport more than one criterion when they are cheap; disagreement between them is\ninformation for the user.\n\n## 3. Write and run the sweep\n\nAdapt `reference/lcurve_template.py`:\n\n- replace `build_problem` with the user's operator, data and regularisation;\n- replace `solve(eps, x0)` with the user's solver call **with everything else\n  fixed** (same `niter`, tolerance, `x0` policy);\n- use a **log-spaced grid** spanning at least 4 orders of magnitude around a\n  scale-based guess (e.g. `ε₀ ≈ ‖Opᴴ y‖ / ‖Rᴴ R Opᴴ y‖` for quadratic terms, or\n  `eps₀ ≈ max|Opᴴ y|` scale for L1 thresholds, where larger values give all-zero\n  models);\n- solve from large to small ε and **warm start** from the previous solution\n  (`x0=...`) to save iterations - unless the solver ignores `x0`;\n- keep `niter` large enough that each solve has converged, otherwise the curve\n  measures iteration count, not regularisation (check a couple of cost curves);\n- for early stopping as regulariser, run once with a callback that stores\n  `x` every k iterations and compute the same criteria on iteration number.\n\nRun it, then look at the plot before trusting the automatic pick: an L-curve with\nno clear corner (common with L1 terms or very noisy data) should be reported as\nsuch, not forced. With derivative regularisers a second knee often appears at\nvery large ε (the model collapses into the null space of `R`, e.g. a linear\ntrend for `SecondDerivative`); it bends the other way and is not the L-curve\ncorner – the template's curvature orientation already excludes it.\n\n## 4. Hand off\n\nReport:\n\n- the parameter swept, the range and the number of solves;\n- the value picked by each criterion and the one recommended, with the reason;\n- the plot file(s) (L-curve, residual vs ε, SNR vs ε if available) and the\n  model at the chosen value next to one over- and one under-regularised model;\n- the exact line to change in the user's inversion script.\n\n\nARGUMENTS: User has a PyLops regularized_inversion with Op = Convolve1D, D2 = SecondDerivative, epsRs=[1.0] picked by eye, result looks too smooth. Wants an L-curve for epsRs and a recommended value."}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"87904353-5187-49c4-8ea3-cf323135c786","uuid":"519a08df-6bed-48bc-8135-347fd2b695c8","timestamp":"2026-10-08T22:38:10.465Z","isSynthetic":true}

If there\nis none, use `solve-inverse-problem` first.\n\n## 0. Check the environment\n\nSame as the other PyLops skills: use the project's Python runner, check\n`pylops.__version__`, inspect solver signatures in the installed version.\n\n## 1. Identify the parameter(s)\n\nMap the user's solver to the knob being tuned and its meaning:\n\n| Solver | Parameter | Objective term |\n|---|---|---|\n| `cgls` / `lsqr` | `damp` | `damp² ‖x‖²` |\n| `regularized_inversion` | `epsRs=[ε₁, ε₂, …]` | `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` |\n| `normal_equations_inversion` | `epsI`, `epsRs` (and `epsNRs`) | `epsI² ‖x‖²`, `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` (same scaling as `regularized_inversion`) |\n| `ista` / `fista` | `eps` | `eps ‖SOpᴴ x‖₁` (effective threshold is `eps·alpha/2`) |\n| `splitbregman` | `epsRL1s`, `epsRL2s`, `mu` | L1 and L2 weights, data weight |\n| `spgl1` | `sigma` (BPDN) or `tau` (LASSO) | residual bound or L1-ball radius |\n| any Krylov solver without explicit regularisation | `niter` | early stopping (semi-convergence) |\n\nWith more than one weight, tune one at a time (fix the others at a\nscale-based guess), or fix their ratio and sweep an overall factor. Do not\nlaunch a full grid over several parameters unless the problem is small.\n\n## 2. Pick the criterion\n\nChoose based on what the user knows:\n\n1. **Known noise level** (sigma per sample, or an estimate from a noise-only\n   window): **discrepancy principle** - choose the largest regularisation whose\n   data residual `‖y − Op x‖` is ≤ `τ · σ · sqrt(N)`, `τ ≈ 1.0–1.1`. For\n   `spgl1`, this is exactly `sigma = τ σ sqrt(N)`.\n2. **Synthetic with a true model**: maximise `pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, x)`\n   (report it, but warn it does not transfer 1:1 to field data).\n3. **Nothing known**: **L-curve** - plot `log ‖Rx‖` vs `log ‖y − Op x‖` and pick the\n   point of maximum curvature. Needs a wide enough range to show both branches.\n4. **Small problem** (`Op.todense()` affordable, N, M ≲ 3000) and quadratic\n   regularisation: **GCV** computed exactly on the dense matrices (implemented in\n   the template).\n\nReport more than one criterion when they are cheap; disagreement between them is\ninformation for the user.\n\n## 3. Write and run the sweep\n\nAdapt `reference/lcurve_template.py`:\n\n- replace `build_problem` with the user's operator, data and regularisation;\n- replace `solve(eps, x0)` with the user's solver call **with everything else\n  fixed** (same `niter`, tolerance, `x0` policy);\n- use a **log-spaced grid** spanning at least 4 orders of magnitude around a\n  scale-based guess (e.g. `ε₀ ≈ ‖Opᴴ y‖ / ‖Rᴴ R Opᴴ y‖` for quadratic terms, or\n  `eps₀ ≈ max|Opᴴ y|` scale for L1 thresholds, where larger values give all-zero\n  models);\n- solve from large to small ε and **warm start** from the previous solution\n  (`x0=...`) to save iterations - unless the solver ignores `x0`;\n- keep `niter` large enough that each solve has converged, otherwise the curve\n  measures iteration count, not regularisation (check a couple of cost curves);\n- for early stopping as regulariser, run once with a callback that stores\n  `x` every k iterations and compute the same criteria on iteration number.\n\nRun it, then look at the plot before trusting the automatic pick: an L-curve with\nno clear corner (common with L1 terms or very noisy data) should be reported as\nsuch, not forced. With derivative regularisers a second knee often appears at\nvery large ε (the model collapses into the null space of `R`, e.g. a linear\ntrend for `SecondDerivative`); it bends the other way and is not the L-curve\ncorner – the template's curvature orientation already excludes it.\n\n## 4. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /private/tmp/e-wcXhsJ/tmp/claude-501/bundled-skills/2.1.295/d317d2befc8628718169e903ed23108f/dataviz\n\n# Data Visualization\n\nA chart is **read by people and executed by you**. This skill turns \"make it look\ngood\" into a procedure with checks, so the result is right by construction rather\nthan by taste.\n\n**The method here is design-system-agnostic.** Nothing in the procedure, the form\nheuristic, the six checks, or the mark specs is specific to one product. A design\nsystem supplies a small set of *parameters* (its ramps, a categorical order, a\ndiverging pair, a status palette, a texture, its surfaces, its filter components);\nthe method consumes them unchanged. A **validated default palette** is the\nreference instance, fully specified in `references/palette.md`. To target your\nbrand, read that file's structure and substitute its values - touch nothing else.\n\n> The single most important habit: **the color part is computable, so compute it.**\n> Never eyeball whether a palette is colorblind-safe - run `scripts/validate_palette.js`.\n\n## The procedure - do these in order\n\nColor comes LAST. Most bad charts pick colors first.\n\n1. **Pick the form.** What is the data's job - magnitude, identity, polarity, a\n   single headline, change-over-time? The job picks the chart type, and sometimes\n   the answer is *not a chart* (a stat tile or hero number). -> `references/choosing-a-form.md`\n2. **Assign color by the job it does.** Categorical (identity), sequential\n   (magnitude), diverging (polarity), or status (state) - each has one rule.\n   Assign categorical hues in fixed order, never cycled. -> `references/color-formula.md`\n3. **VALIDATE the palette - run the script, don't reason about Delta E.**\n   `node scripts/validate_palette.js \"<hex,hex,...>\" --mode light` (relative to\n   this skill's base directory - or load it as `<script type=\"module\">` in the\n   chart's own page, where it reads\n   `data-palette` off `<body>` and logs a `console.table` report). It returns\n   pass/fail on the lightness band, chroma floor, adjacent-pair CVD separation,\n   the normal-vision floor, and contrast. Fix anything that FAILs before continuing. Re-run for\n   `--mode dark` with that mode's surface.\n4. **Apply mark specs & spacers.** Thin marks, 4px rounded data-ends anchored to\n   the baseline, 2px lines, >=8px markers, a 2px surface gap between fills (stacked\n   segments and adjacent bars alike) and a 2px surface ring on overlapping marks,\n   selective direct labels. -> `references/marks-and-anatomy.md`\n5. **Add the hover layer - by default.** An HTML/SVG chart *is* interactive; ship\n   a crosshair+tooltip on line/area and a per-mark hover tooltip on bar/dot/cell.\n   The only form that skips it is a bare stat tile with no plot. Hit targets bigger\n   than the mark; filters in one row above the charts. -> `references/interaction.md`\n6. **Final accessibility pass.** For >= 2 series a legend is always present and <= 4\n   are also direct-labeled (a single series needs no legend box - the title names\n   it), so identity is never color-alone; a table view exists; dark mode is **selected** - its own\n   steps from the same ramps, validated against the dark surface, not an automatic\n   flip; texture is available for the CVD/print/forced-colors case.\n7. **Render it and look at it.** The validator checks color, not layout - open or\n   screenshot the output and eyeball it for label collisions, geometry, and overflow\n   before calling it done.\n\nThen check the result against **`references/anti-patterns.md`** - it is the catalog\nof what goes wrong. If your chart matches an entry, it's wrong.\n\n## Non-negotiables (true in every design system)\n\n- **Assign categorical hues in fixed order, never cycled.** A 9th series is never a\n  generated hue - it folds into \"Other,\" small multiples, or composite encoding.\n- **One axis.** Never a dual-axis chart (two y-scales). Two measures of different\n  scale -> two charts, small multiples, or indexed to a common base. *(This is the\n  #1 chart mistake - see anti-patterns.)*\n- **Color follows the entity, never its rank.** A filter that changes the series\n  count must not repaint the survivors.\n- **Sequential = one hue, light->dark. Diverging = two hues + a neutral gray\n  midpoint.** Never a rainbow; never a hue at the diverging midpoint.\n- **Run the validator before shipping any categorical palette.** CVD Delta E >= 8 is the\n  target (OKLab ×100); 6-8 is a floor that is legal ONLY with secondary encoding. A\n  normal-vision floor below 15 is a hard FAIL - full-color readers can't tell the\n  pair apart; re-step it on the adjacent pairlist (secondary encoding does not excuse\n  this one); under `--pairs all` cut series or facet instead - see check 4. A contrast WARN\n  obligates visible labels or a table view - it is not dismissable.\n- **Thin marks; a legend always present for >= 2 series (none for one), with\n  selective direct labels (never a number on every point); recessive grid/axes.**\n- **Text wears text tokens, never the series color** - values, labels, and legends\n  stay in primary/secondary/muted ink; a colored mark beside them carries identity.\n- **Status colors are reserved** (good/warning/serious/critical) and never reused\n  for \"series 4\"; they ship with an icon + label, never color alone.\n\n## Plugging in a design system\n\nThe method is invariant; only these parameters change per system. The reference\ninstance - every value filled in - is `references/palette.md`.\n\n| Parameter | What the system provides |\n|---|---|\n| **Ramps** | the hue scales (named steps) the palette draws from |\n| **Categorical theme** | the fixed hue order (a named theme); default + alternates |\n| **Sequential hue** | the default single hue for magnitude |\n| **Diverging pair** | two warm/cool poles + a neutral midpoint |\n| **Status palette** | good / warning / serious / critical - steps distinct from categorical |\n| **Texture fill** | one directional hand-drawn fill, used at 45° / 135° |\n| **Surfaces** | light & dark chart-surface colors (the validator needs these) |\n| **Filter controls** | date-range & dimension controls (behavioral spec in `interaction.md`) |\n\nTo onboard a new system: fill those rows, feed its ramps to the validator, and let\nit snap each slot to the nearest passing step. Structure and rules stay as written.\n\n## Reference files\n\n| File | What it answers |\n|------|-----------------|\n| `references/choosing-a-form.md` | Which chart type / is it even a chart? |\n| `references/color-formula.md` | The four jobs, the six checks, snap-to-passing |\n| `references/marks-and-anatomy.md` | Mark specs, spacers, labels, figures, hero number |\n| `references/interaction.md` | Tooltips & hover, filters & time ranges |\n| `references/components.md` | The pieces a chart is made of - build each in plain HTML |\n| `references/anti-patterns.md`

Skill called 0x (expected 0..0)

Prompt

My smooth-model inversion with PyLops works fine, but I picked epsRs=[1.0] by eye and the result looks a bit too smooth. How should I choose epsRs properly? Please make me an L-curve for it and tell me which value to use. Here's the script:

import numpy as np
import pylops

rng = np.random.default_rng(5)
n = 200
z = np.arange(n)
xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * z / 80) + 0.5 * np.exp(-0.5 * ((z - 120) / 6) ** 2)
t = np.arange(-15, 16)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 4) ** 2); h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=15)
y = Op @ xtrue + 0.02 * rng.standard_normal(n)

D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(n)
xinv = pylops.regularized_inversion(Op, y, [D2], epsRs=[1.0], iter_lim=200)[0]
print(np.linalg.norm(xinv - xtrue) / np.linalg.norm(xtrue))
